Compiling to recurrent neurons CCS: Theory of computation Type theory CCS: Software and its engineering Compilers CCS: Theory of computation Denotational semantics CCS: Computing methodologies Machine learning
Joey Velez-Ginorio, Nada Amin, Konrad Paul Kording, Steve Zdancewic

Abstract
. Discrete structures are currently second-class in differentiable programming. Since functions over discrete structures lack overt derivatives, differentiable programs do not differentiate through them and limit where they can be used. For example, when programming a neural network, conditionals and iteration cannot be used everywhere; they can break the derivatives necessary for gradient-based learning to work. This limits the class of differentiable algorithms we can directly express, imposing restraints on how we build neural networks and differentiable programs more generally. However, these restraints are not fundamental. Recent work shows conditionals can be first-class, by compiling them into differentiable form as linear neurons. Similarly, this work shows iteration can be first-class—by compiling to linear recurrent neurons. We present a minimal typed, higher-order and linear programming language with iteration called Cajal ( ⊸ , 𝟚 , ℕ ) {\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})} . We prove its programs compile correctly to recurrent neurons, allowing discrete algorithms to be expressed in a differentiable form compatible with gradient-based learning. With our implementation, we conduct two experiments where we link these recurrent neurons against a neural network solving an iterative image transformation task. This determines part of its function prior to learning. As a result, the network learns faster and with greater data-efficiency relative to a neural network programmed without first-class iteration. A key lesson is that recurrent neurons enable a rich interplay between learning and the discrete structures of ordinary programming.



1.
Introduction

Figure 1
.
Iterative image transform

Functions over discrete structures lack overt derivatives. Nevertheless, they could be differentiated if compiled to differentiable form, enabling discrete structure to appear anywhere in a differentiable program. But it is not yet clear how to build these compilers. For conditionals, recent work shows it is possible by compiling programs to linear neurons
1
1

1

Linear neurons are essentially linear maps.

(
Velez-Ginorio et al. 2026
)
. For iteration over natural numbers, we build on those efforts to show it is also possible---by compiling programs to linear recurrent neurons.

2

2

Linear recurrent neurons are essentially linear dynamical systems.
This allows us to directly express differentiable algorithms with discrete iterative structure. For example, consider the following image transformation in Fig.

.

Recognizing whether a digit is

. Therefore it seems a neural network could not be programmed with this algorithm, as the derivatives necessary for gradient-based learning would be absent for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}
.

But it is possible. With
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
, a neural network can be programmed, as in Fig.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}0}

Compiling iteration into differentiable form

Programming and compiling an iterative image transform

Prior work identifies linear programming languages as promising source languages for such compilers
(
Velez-Ginorio et al. 2026
)
. Their semantic connection to linear algebra opens a route towards differentiating discrete structure, by compiling linear programs to linear maps between
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}}
-vector spaces. However, while the case of conditionals is clear, the differentiable status of iteration is more subtle. In prior work, the compiled linear maps were between
finite
-dimensional
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}}
-vector spaces, which are unambiguously differentiable
(
Spivak 2018
)
. For iteration, the compiled linear maps can be between
infinite
-dimensional
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}}
-vector spaces—and without additional assumption, these linear maps may not be differentiable
(
Kreyszig 1991
)
.

Fortunately, an assumption is common when programming recurrent neurons: you differentiate with respect to a finite-dimensional
subspace
. For example, we show that the program in Fig.
2
compiles to the recurrent neural network in Fig.
2
, which can be implemented as a differentiable program (Section
5
). The critical observation is that the recurrence
if observed finitely
, renders a linear recurrent neuron equivalent to a linear map between finite-dimensional
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}}
-vector spaces.

With
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
, our aim is to present these essential issues in plain terms, using a minimal calculus whose formal specification and metatheory are tractable and pleasing to explore. We are interested in an empirical
and
mathematical method for giving discrete structure first-class status in differentiable programming. Specifically, our contributions are:

\triangleright

We specify the syntax, evaluation, and typing of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
programs (Section
4.1
).

\triangleright

We prove that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
programs compile correctly to recurrent neurons (Section
4.2
).

\triangleright

We implement
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
, programming a neural network to solve the iterative transform task (Fig.
1
) and a conditional variant using first-class conditionals and iteration (Section
5
).

These findings suggest that more complex forms of recurrence in neural networks could implement richer forms of differentiable primitive recursion—over lists, trees, and arbitrary algebraic data (Section
7
).

2.
Linear recurrent neurons

Linear recurrent neurons are discrete-time linear dynamical systems
(
Antsaklis and Michel 2006
)
. They transform vectors using linear maps, but are additionally parameterized by
time
. For example, consider the following system whose input is a timestep
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}}
, and whose output is a vector
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}v}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}^{2}}
.

{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}0})={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}}\quad\quad{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n+1})={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0&2\\
3&0\end{bmatrix}}\Bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n})\Bigr)

We can imagine this system evolving over time by enumerating in sequence its
state
at each
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}
.

\Biggl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
3\end{bmatrix}},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}6\\
0\end{bmatrix}},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
18\end{bmatrix}},\cdots\Biggr)

It is a
linear
system because each transition is determined by a matrix multiplication; the next state is a linear combination of its previous state. And it is a
discrete-time
system because time is parameterized by the natural numbers.

Equivalently, we may view the system as a linear recurrent neural network with two recurrent neurons, shown in Fig.
3
. The input is the initial state determined by
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}0})
. The recurrent connections between the two neurons
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}h_{0}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}h_{1}}
are determined by the coefficients of the matrix in
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n+1})
. Its output at any timestep
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}
is given by
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}y}={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n})
. Importantly, when unfolding the dynamics up to a particular timestep
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}
, as in Fig.
3
, recurrent neurons become equivalent to an ordinary linear neural network. They are linear and differentiable maps in the initial state
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}0})
.

{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}h_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}}
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}h_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}}

Linear recurrent neurons and their dynamics

However, observe that
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}
is not overtly differentiable in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}
. The derivative should describe how small changes to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}
impact the behavior of
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}
. But natural numbers, as in other discrete structure, lack an adequate notion of
small changes
. As a result, there is no overt derivative with respect to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}
. Yet there is recourse. Consider the following function
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}
whose input is an infinite sequence of real numbers
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}x_{i}}
with finite support, and whose output is a vector
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}v}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}^{2}}
.

{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}x})={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}}}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}x}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0&2\\
3&0\end{bmatrix}^{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}}\Bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}}\Bigr)

First, observe that
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}
is linear in
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}x}
.

\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}3}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\!\!&\!\!2\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!\cdots\end{bmatrix}})\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}+}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}4}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\!\!&\!\!3\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!\cdots\end{bmatrix}})={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}3\cdot\begin{bmatrix}1\!\!&\!\!2\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!\cdots\end{bmatrix}\;+\;4\cdot\begin{bmatrix}0\!\!&\!\!3\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!\cdots\end{bmatrix}})

Next, if we restrict
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}x}
to be a
finite
3
3

3

The finiteness assumption is necessary for the notion of differentiation to be canonical.
sequence, then
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}
is also differentiable in
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}x}
. For example, consider the restriction of
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}
to sequences of length
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}3}
.

{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}_{\mid{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}3}}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}7\!\!&\!\!8\!\!&\!\!9\end{bmatrix}})={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}7}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0&2\\
3&0\end{bmatrix}^{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}0}}\Bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}}\Bigr)\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}+}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}8}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0&2\\
3&0\end{bmatrix}^{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}1}}\Bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}}\Bigr)\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}+}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}9}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0&2\\
3&0\end{bmatrix}^{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}2}}\Bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}}\Bigr)={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}61\\
24\end{bmatrix}}

From
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}
we obtain a linear map
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}_{\mid{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}3}}:{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}}
. The meaning of a small change to its input is standard. From the above example, you can imagine how these changes would impact its behavior. But the most remarkable property of
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}_{\mid{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}3}}
is that, with appropriate choice of
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}x}
, it
exactly
implements the system
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}
up to its first three states as a linear and differentiable map.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}_{\mid{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}3}}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!0\end{bmatrix}})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}1}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0&2\\
3&0\end{bmatrix}^{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}0}}\Bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}}\Bigr)\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}+}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0&2\\
3&0\end{bmatrix}^{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}1}}\Bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}}\Bigr)\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}+}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0&2\\
3&0\end{bmatrix}^{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}2}}\Bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\\

0\end{bmatrix}}={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}0})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}_{\mid{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}3}}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\!\!&\!\!1\!\!&\!\!0\end{bmatrix}})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0&2\\
3&0\end{bmatrix}^{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}0}}\Bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}}\Bigr)\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}+}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}1}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0&2\\
3&0\end{bmatrix}^{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}1}}\Bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}}\Bigr)\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}+}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0&2\\
3&0\end{bmatrix}^{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}2}}\Bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}}\Bigr)={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
3\end{bmatrix}}={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}1})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}_{\mid{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}3}}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!1\end{bmatrix}})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0&2\\
3&0\end{bmatrix}^{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}0}}\Bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\\

This is the essential theory underlying how we compile iteration in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
. In fact,
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}g}_{\mid{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}3}}
implements (modulo rescaling) the logical operator
not
iterated over
true
(Section
4
).

3.
Linear programs

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
is a linearly typed programming language. Linear types restrict how variables are used in a program
(
Walker 2005
)
. They carefully permit the duplication of variables and forbid the discarding of variables. Curiously, these restrictions are the basis for their connection to linear algebra
(
Mellies 2009
)
. It is what links
linear
programs and
linear
maps.

⬇

tru

x

=

x

then

True

True

(b)

Programming linearly

Figure 4
.
Programming with and without duplicating/discarding
x

Consider the linear and nonlinear version of
tru
in Fig.
4
. Both implement a constant function which always return true. However, observe that the nonlinear version in Fig.
4
duplicates
x
in the else branch of the conditional. If applied to an argument,
tru
uses
x
when checking the condition
and
to return a value in the else branch. But the linear version in Fig.
4
only uses
x
once when checking the condition. Additionally, observe that the nonlinear version in Fig.
4
does not use
x
, which is in scope, when returning true in the if branch. But the linear version in Fig.
4
uses everything in scope, as
x
is not in scope in either branch.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}x\!:\!\mathbb{2}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{2}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}x\!:\!\mathbb{2}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textsf{tt}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{2}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}x\!:\!\mathbb{2}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{2}}}

✗

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}x\!:\!\mathbb{2}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textsf{if}\;\underline{x}\;\textsf{then}\;\underline{\textsf{tt}}\;\textsf{else}\;\underline{x}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{2}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varnothing}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda x.\textsf{if}\;\underline{x}\;\textsf{then}\;\underline{\textsf{tt}}\;\textsf{else}\;\underline{x}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{2}\to\mathbb{2}}}

(a)

Typing nonlinearly

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}x\!:\!\mathbb{2}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{2}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varnothing}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textsf{tt}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{2}}}

✓

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}x\!:\!\mathbb{2}}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textsf{if}\;\underline{x}\;\textsf{then}\;\underline{\textsf{tt}}\;\textsf{else}\;\underline{\textsf{tt}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{2}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varnothing}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda x.\textsf{if}\;\underline{x}\;\textsf{then}\;\underline{\textsf{tt}}\;\textsf{else}\;\underline{\textsf{tt}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{2}\multimap\mathbb{2}}}

(b)

Typing linearly

Figure 5
.
Typing with and without duplicating/discarding
x

Typing can detect these distinctions. The nonlinear typing in Fig.
5
duplicates
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}x\!:\!\mathbb{2}}
when typing the conditional; it duplicates its context across each subprogram. But the linear typing in Fig.
5
does not duplicate
{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}x\!:\!\mathbb{2}}
when typing the conditional. Its context is
split
across each subprogram. Additionally, the nonlinear typing in Fig.
5
discards the variable
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}x\!:\!\mathbb{2}}
when typing
tt
in the then branch of the conditional. However, by splitting the context, the linear typing in Fig.
5
does not discard anything.

Generally, linear typing must ensure the structural rules of
contraction
and
weakening
are not permissible
(
Walker 2005
)
. Their impermissibility establish an important link between linear programs and linear maps, a connection we can use to differentiate through discrete structure.

4.
Cajal
({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})

\textsf{iter}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{not}\,x}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}2})
\mathsf{ff}
\mathsf{tt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\displaystyle\sum\limits_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}}}}}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
1\\
0\\
\vdots\end{bmatrix}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\!\!\!&&\!\!\!1\\
1\!\!\!&&\!\!\!0\end{bmatrix}^{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}}\Bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}}\Bigr)\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\\

\scriptstyle\not\Downarrow

\scriptstyle\Downarrow

\scriptstyle\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}-}\rrbracket

Figure 6
.
Correctly compiling to recurrent neurons

To enable first-class iteration in differentiable programming, a
correct
compiler should establish a correspondence between evaluation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Downarrow}
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
programs and equations underlying the dynamics of linear recurrent neurons. Fig.
6
details this notion of correctness, equivalent to establishing the
soundness
and
adequacy
of a denotational semantics
(
Winskel 1993
)
. Establishing this correspondence is the central focus of this section. It sets the stage for differentiable programming with first-class iteration, where we program neural networks with discrete iterative structure in a differentiable way (Section
5
).

4.1.
Defining

Syntax

\begin{aligned} &\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Expression}}\\[8.53581pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\,\,\,\coloneqq\ &\ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}\in\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}Var}}&&\textsf{variable}\\
&\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}&&\textsf{true}\\
&\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{ff}}&&\textsf{false}\\
&\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}&&\textsf{zero}\\
&\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(e)}&&\textsf{successor}\\
&\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{iter}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}y}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{3}})}&&\textsf{iterator}\\
&\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\lambda x.e}&&\textsf{linear map}\\
&\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}e_{2}}&&\textsf{linear application}\\
\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} &\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Type}}\\[8.53581pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}\,\,\,\coloneqq&\ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}}&&\textsf{boolean}\\
&\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}}&&\textsf{natural number}\\
&\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau_{1}\multimap\tau_{2}}&&\textsf{linear map}\\
\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} &\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Value}}\\[8.53581pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\,\,\,\coloneqq&\ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}&&\textsf{true}\\
&\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{ff}}&&\textsf{false}\\
&\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}&&\textsf{zero}\\
&\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(v)}&&\textsf{successor}\\
&\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\lambda x.e}&&\textsf{linear map}\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} &\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Context}}\\[8.53581pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}\,\,\,\coloneqq&\ \ \ {\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\varnothing}&&\textsf{empty}\\
&\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta,x\!:\!\tau}&&\textsf{binder}\\
\end{aligned}

Figure 7
.
Syntax of
Cajal
({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})

4.1.1.
Syntax

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
is a minimal typed, higher-order and linear programming language. Fig.
7
details its syntax, similar to Gödel’s
System T
of higher-order primitive recursion
(
Harper 2016
)
. The main differences are the use of iterators (rather than recursors) for natural numbers and inclusion of a boolean type. These distinctions are inessential to the underlying theory, but simplify the presentation of certain ideas. When convenient, instead of explicit successors, we use an ordinary syntax for natural numbers. For example, using
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}2}
instead of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}})})}
.

Evaluating

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\iff\textsf{expression }{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\textsf{ evaluates to }{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(e)}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(v)}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\lambda x.e}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v_{2}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\{x\mapsto v_{2}\}(e)}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}e_{2}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v_{2}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{if}\;e_{1}\;\textsf{then}\;e_{2}\;\textsf{else}\;e_{3}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v_{2}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{ff}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{3}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v_{3}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{if}\;e_{1}\;\textsf{then}\;e_{2}\;\textsf{else}\;e_{3}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v_{3}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v_{1}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{3}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{iter}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}y}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{3}})}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v_{1}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{iter}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}y}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v_{3}})}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v_{\textsf{n}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\{y\mapsto v_{\textsf{n}}\}(e_{2})}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{3}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(v_{\textsf{3}})}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{iter}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}y}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{3}})}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}

Figure 8
.
Evaluating
Cajal
({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})
programs

4.1.2.
Evaluating

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
expressions evaluate according to the
big-step
evaluation relation in Fig.
8
; it specifies a call-by-value interpreter for the language. The notation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\{x\mapsto v\}(e)}
describes a capture-avoiding substitution, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}
is replaced by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}
in the expression
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}
. In general, evaluation is similar to a simply-typed lambda calculus, with iteration as an exception. To clarify, consider the following iterator which doubles
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}1}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}2}
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{iter}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}y}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(y)})}}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}})}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}})}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}})}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\{y\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(y)})})}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}})})}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}})}}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}})}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{iter}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}y}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(y)})}}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}})}})}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}})})}}

The iterator dispatches on the value of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{3}}
. If it is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}0}
, it returns the value of the base case
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}
. Otherwise, if it is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}
then the recurrence
({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}y\hookrightarrow e_{2}})
will apply
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}
times over the base case.

Typing

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}\iff\textsf{program }{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\textsf{ has type }{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}

\mathord{}\mathord{}

\mathord{}\mathord{}

.
Typing
Cajal
({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})
programs

4.1.3.
Typing

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
is linearly typed. The presence of context splitting in typing application, conditionals, and iteration ensures that the structural rule
contraction
is not permissible. This relation relates two contexts if their variable names are disjoint. For example,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}(x\!:\!\mathbb{2},y\!:\!\mathbb{2})\circ(z\!:\!\mathbb{2})}

inhabits the relation, but

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}(x\!:\!\mathbb{2},y\!:\!\mathbb{2})\circ(y\!:\!\mathbb{2})}

does not (Appendix A.1). Separately, the demand of singleton or empty contexts in typing variables or constants ensures that the structural rule
weakening
is not permissible.

There is now a key property we must prove about typing—well typed programs must have behavior. Otherwise, we cannot establish a correspondence with equations underlying the dynamics of recurrent neurons. Later, it helps us prove our analog of
adequacy
for a denotational semantics.

Theorem

1:
(Programs evaluate to values)

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\varnothing}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}

, then

\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}

Proof sketch.
The argument is similar to
logical relations
proofs of termination for typed lambda calculi. Appendix B.1 contains a detailed proof. In short, the argument shows that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
programs inhabit the logical relation shown in Fig.
10
, and that programs in this logical relation terminate.

Logical Relation

\begin{aligned} &\;\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{V}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\_}\rrbracket:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Type}}\to\Big\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Value}}\Big\}\\[8.53581pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{V}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}}\rrbracket&=\Big\{\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{ff}}\;\Big\}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{V}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}}\rrbracket&=\Big\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}}\end{array}\Big\}\cup\Big\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(v)}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{V}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}}\rrbracket\end{array}\Big\}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{V}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau_{1}\multimap\tau_{2}}\rrbracket&=\Big\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\lambda x.e}\mid\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{V}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau_{1}}\rrbracket,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}(\lambda x.e)v}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{E}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau_{2}}\rrbracket\\
\end{array}\Big\}\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} {\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\_}\rrbracket:&\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Context}}\to\Big\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Environment}}\Big\}\\[8.53581pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\varnothing}\rrbracket&=\Big\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\varnothing}\Big\}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta,x\!:\!\tau}\rrbracket&=\Big\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\sigma\{x\mapsto v\}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\sigma}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}\rrbracket,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{V}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}\rrbracket\end{array}\Big\}\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} {\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{E}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\_}\rrbracket:&\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Type}}\to\Big\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Expression}}\Big\}\\[8.53581pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{E}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}\rrbracket&=\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\;\middle|\;\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v},\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{V}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}\rrbracket\\
\end{array}\right\}\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} \underline{\textsf{Logical Typing}}&\\[5.69054pt]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}\vDash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}&\iff\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\sigma}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}\rrbracket,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\sigma(e)}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{E}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}\rrbracket\end{aligned}

Figure 10
.
Logical Relation for
Cajal
({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})

At each type, the logical relation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{E}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}\rrbracket
returns a set of terminating
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
expressions. It includes expressions which are
not
syntactically well-typed. For example,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}(\textsf{if}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}\;\textsf{then}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}\;\textsf{else}\;\underline{0})}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathcal{R}_{\textsf{E}}}\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}}\rrbracket
. We say these expression are
logically
well-typed,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\varnothing}\vDash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{if}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}\;\textsf{then}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}\;\textsf{else}\;\underline{{0}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}}
. To show termination for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
, we first prove by induction on typing derivations that syntactically well-typed programs can be logically typed.

Lemma

1:
(Programs can be logically typed)

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}

, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}\vDash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}

The bulk of the effort is proving that programs can be logically typed. Afterwards, it is immediate from the definition of the logical relation that logically typed programs terminate.

Lemma

2:
(Logically typed programs evaluate to values)

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}\vDash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}

, then

\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\sigma(e)}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}

From these lemmas, it follows that syntactically well-typed programs in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
evaluate to values. In other words, they have behavior. Our efforts may now shift toward implementing these behaviors with the dynamics of recurrent neurons.

4.2.
Compiling

4.2.1.
Types

The first step in compiling
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Cajal}}{\scriptstyle({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})}
is to compile its types to
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}}
-vector spaces. To implement a type with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}
distinct
4
4

4

Distinct meaning not contextually equivalent. For example,
\lambda x.x
and
\lambda y.y
are contextually equivalent.
values, an
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}
-dimensional space generally suffices. For finite data like booleans, the choice of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}
-dimensional space is simple.
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}^{2}}
suffices to implement boolean programs as linear maps, shown in Fig.
11
. Note that if the target vector space has too few dimensions, then a linear map that adequately implements a program
may not exist
.

Compiling

τ

\begin{aligned} \llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\_}\rrbracket:&\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Type}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\textsf{Vector Space}}\\[8.00003pt]
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}}\rrbracket&={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}^{2}}\\[2.84526pt]
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}}\rrbracket&=\bigl\{{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}x}:{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}}\mid\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\textsf{supp}}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}x})={\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}\bigr\}\\[2.84526pt]
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau_{1}\multimap\tau_{2}}\rrbracket&={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\textsf{Lin}}(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau_{1}}\rrbracket,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau_{2}}\rrbracket)\\
\end{aligned}

Figure 11
.
Compiling
Cajal
({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})
types

For natural numbers, the choice of vector space is more subtle. In Fig.
11
, natural numbers compile to an infinite-dimensional sequence space. Each vector is an infinite-length sequence, represented as a function which yields the value of the sequence at any index
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n\in\mathbb{N}}
. The support of these functions is finite—only a finite number of entries in a sequence may be non-zero. This assumption is critical because our strategy for compiling iteration involves a summation over sequences, and this summation is not well-defined without finite support. To form a vector space, vector addition and scalar multiplication are defined pointwise.

For functions, we compile to the vector space of linear maps between the compiled input and output types. To form a vector space, vector addition and scalar multiplication are defined pointwise. Our approach differs from prior work studying first-class conditionals in differentiable programming, where functions compiled to matrix spaces
(
Velez-Ginorio et al. 2026
)
. As we developed our metatheory and implementation, we found it much simpler to represent function types abstractly as linear maps. If necessary, the matrix of a linear map can be determined in a separate compiler pass.

4.2.2.
Contexts

Programs with free variables require us to compile contexts, shown in Fig.
12
. These compiled contexts specify which vectors we can link compiled neurons against. Generally, a context with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}k}
variables compiles to a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}k}
-tuple of vectors.
5
5

5

By convention we denote elements of a compiled
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}1}
-tuple using
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{x}}
, and elements of a compiled
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}k}
-tuple as
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{\sigma}}
.

Compiling

Δ

\begin{aligned} \llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\_}\rrbracket:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Context}}&\to{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\textsf{Tuple}}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\textsf{Vector}})\\[8.00003pt]
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\varnothing}\rrbracket&={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\{0\}}\\[2.84526pt]
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta,x\!:\!\tau}\rrbracket&=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}\rrbracket\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\times}\;\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}\rrbracket\\
\end{aligned}

Figure 12
.
Compiling
Cajal
({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})
contexts

4.2.3.
Programs

Using a compiler for contexts and types, we compile typing judgments
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}
to multilinear maps from
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}\rrbracket
to
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}\rrbracket
. Generally, a typing judgment with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}k}
variables in the context will compile to a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}k}
-linear map. The compiler is a function defined recursively on the structure of typing derivations, shown in Fig.
13
. Prior work on first-class conditionals used a relational specification. However, we find a functional specification leads to simpler proofs and a simpler implementation.

Compiling

e

\begin{aligned} &\hskip 130.0002pt\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}-}\rrbracket:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{Program}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\textsf{Multilinear map}}\\[8.00003pt]

\\

programs

Compiling variables is straight-forward. The variable rule maps to identity at each type.

\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x\!:\!\mathbb{2}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}}}\rrbracket\mapsto({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{x}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{x}})

Our specification in Fig.
13
uses an equivalent, less verbose notation. Moreover, we often omit specifying the domain of these multilinear maps. The domain is always
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}\rrbracket
.

The constants
tt
,
ff
, and
0
compile to constant
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}0}
-linear maps. Their domain is
\{{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}\}
and they are vacuously linear in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}0}
arguments. These
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}0}
-linear maps generally map
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}
to orthonormal bases in
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}\rrbracket
. These are so-called
one-hot
encodings. Observe that for natural numbers these encodings occur
in time
.

\displaystyle\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\varnothing}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!\cdots\end{bmatrix}}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\varnothing}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}})}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\!\!&\!\!1\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!\cdots\end{bmatrix}}

\displaystyle\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\varnothing}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{0}})})}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!1\!\!&\!\!\cdots\end{bmatrix}}

Using time, a
single
recurrent neuron can implement natural numbers.

The successor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x\!:\!\mathbb{N}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(x)}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}}
compiles to a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}1}
-linear
right-shift
operator on sequences. Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(x)}}
contains a free variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}
, this compiled program can be
linked
with an
environment
. An environment is any element of
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x\!:\!\mathbb{N}}\rrbracket
, and linking is any application of the
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}1}
-linear right-shift operator to these elements, shown in the following example.

\displaystyle\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x\!:\!\mathbb{N}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(x)}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}10\!\!&\!\!20\!\!&\!\!30\!\!&\!\!\cdots\end{bmatrix}})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\!\!&\!\!10\!\!&\!\!20\!\!&\!\!\cdots\end{bmatrix}}

If we bind
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}
using
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\lambda}
, this compiles instead to a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}0}
-linear map which
returns a linear map
.

\displaystyle\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\varnothing}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\lambda x.{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(x)}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}\multimap\mathbb{N}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0})

\displaystyle=\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{x}}\in\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}}\rrbracket\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\!\!&\!\!\vec{x_{0}}\!\!&\!\!\vec{x_{1}}\!\!&\!\!\cdots\end{bmatrix}}\bigr)

Note that our specification in Fig.
13
omits typing information when it is clear from context. For example,
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\rrbracket
is used in the function case. But the compiler is defined on typing derivations, not expressions. This is shorthand for
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta,x\!:\!\tau_{1}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau_{2}}\rrbracket
; the convention is
very
useful in simplifying definitions and proofs.

An application compiles to function application. However, context splitting introduces some subtlety. Observe how the environment
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{y}}

\displaystyle=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\lambda x.{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(x)}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0})(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}y}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{y}}))

Essentially, the environment must be split to mirror context splitting. In Fig.
13
, the notation
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{\sigma}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}
handles this. It defines the restriction of an environment
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{\sigma}}
according to a context
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}
. Appendix A.7 contains a detailed specification.

Conditionals compile to a
soft-branching
conditional. Both branches evaluate, returning a linear combination of their values. It implements ordinary
hard-branching
conditionals, but in a fully differentiable way. Observe how the following example is differentiable in the environment, enabling first-class differentiable programming with conditionals. It is a differentiable
not
operator.

\displaystyle\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x\!:\!\mathbb{2}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{if}\;\underline{x}\;\textsf{then}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{ff}}}\;\textsf{else}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}}\rrbracket\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{1}}&\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{2}}\end{bmatrix}}\bigr)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\pi_{1}}\bigl(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}\rrbracket\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{1}}&\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{2}}\end{bmatrix}}\bigr)\bigr)\hskip 0.50003pt\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\hskip 0.50003pt\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{ff}}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0})\hskip 0.50003pt\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}+}\,\,\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\pi_{1}}\bigl(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}\rrbracket\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{1}}&\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{2}}\end{bmatrix}}\bigr)\bigr)\hskip 0.50003pt\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\hskip 0.50003pt\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0})

\displaystyle\textsf{By compiling}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\pi_{1}}\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{1}}&\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{2}}\end{bmatrix}}\bigr)\hskip 0.50003pt\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0&\!\!\!1\end{bmatrix}}\hskip 0.50003pt\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}+}\,\,\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\pi_{2}}\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{1}}&\!\!\!{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{2}}\end{bmatrix}}\bigr)\hskip 0.50003pt\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1&\!\!\!0\end{bmatrix}}

\displaystyle\textsf{By compiling}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{1}}\hskip 0.50003pt\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0&\!\!\!1\end{bmatrix}}\hskip 0.50003pt\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}+}\,\,\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{2}}\hskip 0.50003pt\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1&\!\!\!0\end{bmatrix}}

\displaystyle\textsf{By projections }{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\pi_{i}}

The functions
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\pi_{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\pi_{2}}
used in Fig.

{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}v}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}^{2}}

Iteration compiles to equations underlying the dynamics of linear recurrent neurons, as in Fig
6
. It computes a linear combination of states in a linear dynamical system. Recall that with particular inputs, these equations
exactly
implement the dynamics of linear recurrent neurons (Section 2). This example shows how to compile the iterated
not
operator from Fig.
6
.

\displaystyle\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\varnothing}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{iter}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{if}\;\underline{x}\;\textsf{then}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{ff}}}\;\textsf{else}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}2})}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}}\rrbracket\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}\;(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}2}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}))({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n})\hskip 0.50003pt\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\hskip 0.50003pt(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{if}\;\underline{x}\;\textsf{then}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{ff}}}\;\textsf{else}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}))^{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n}(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}))

\displaystyle\textsf{By compiling}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!1\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!\cdots\end{bmatrix}_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\hskip 0.50003pt\bigl(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{if}\;\underline{x}\;\textsf{then}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{ff}}}\;\textsf{else}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}}\rrbracket\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}\bigr)\bigr)^{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n}\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1&\!\!\!0\end{bmatrix}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\textsf{By compiling}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!1\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!\cdots\end{bmatrix}_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\hskip 0.50003pt\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{x}}\mapsto\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{if}\;\underline{x}\;\textsf{then}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{ff}}}\;\textsf{else}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}}\rrbracket\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{x}}\bigr)\bigr)^{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n}\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1&\!\!\!0\end{bmatrix}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\textsf{By partial application}

\displaystyle\simeq{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!1\!\!&\!\!0\!\!&\!\!\cdots\end{bmatrix}_{n}}\hskip 0.50003pt\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}^{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n}}\bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\begin{bmatrix}1&\!\!\!0\end{bmatrix}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\textsf{By matrix of a linear map}

Observe that this example satisfies the desired correspondence from Fig.
6
between evaluation and the dynamics of linear recurrent neurons. From now on,
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\rrbracket
is shorthand for
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0})
.

\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{iter}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{if}\;\underline{x}\;\textsf{then}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{ff}}}\;\textsf{else}\;\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}2})}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{tt}}}\rrbracket\neq\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{ff}}}\rrbracket

This correspondence holds for any closed

6

A closed program has no free variables.
program of type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}}
.

Theorem

2:
(Compiler preserves program behavior)

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\varnothing}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{base}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(}\tau{\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}}
,
then

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (a)\;&{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\implies\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\rrbracket\\
(b)\;&{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\;\cancel{\Downarrow}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\implies\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\rrbracket\neq\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\rrbracket\\
\end{aligned}

Proof sketch.
The argument requires showing that the compiler preserves what programs will
(a)
and won’t
(b)
do. These correspond to soundness and adequacy of a denotational semantics. Appendix B.4 contains a detailed proof. In short, the argument for
(a)
is by induction on evaluation. Then
(b)
follows as a corollary of
(a)
and Theorem
4.1.3
.

For
(a)
, the induction on evaluation proceeds on the following lemma, which generalizes the type. This gives a sufficient induction hypothesis.

Lemma

3:
(Compiler preserves what programs will do)

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\varnothing}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}

, then

\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\rrbracket

It is worth considering the iteration case in detail
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{iter}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}y}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{3}})}\Downarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{3}}
evaluates to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(v_{3})}}
. It captures the essential reasoning happening across cases in the proof. We must show
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{iter}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}y}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{3}})}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\rrbracket
.

\begin{aligned} &\,\,\;\;\;\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{iter}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}y}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{3}})}\rrbracket\\[5.0pt]
&={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{3}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n})\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket^{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n}(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\rrbracket)&&\textsf{By compiling}\\
&={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{3}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n})\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket^{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}(n-1)}(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\rrbracket))&&\textsf{Because }{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}^{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}v})={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}^{{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}(n-1)}}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}v}))\\
&={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(v_{3})}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n})\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket^{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}(n-1)}(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\rrbracket))&&\textsf{By induction}\\
&=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(v_{3})}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0})\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket^{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\rrbracket)\hskip 0.50003pt\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}+}\,\,\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus 0}}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{succ}(v_{3})}}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n})\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket^{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}(n-1)}(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\rrbracket))&&\textsf{Because }{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n})={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0})\hskip 0.50003pt\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}+}\,\,\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus 0}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n})\\
&={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket^{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}0}(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\rrbracket)\hskip 0.50003pt\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}+}\,\,\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus 0}}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{v_{3}}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n-1})\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket^{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}(n-1)}(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\rrbracket))&&\textsf{By compiling}\\
&={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus 0}}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{v_{3}}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n-1})\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket^{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}(n-1)}(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\rrbracket))&&\textsf{By scalar multiplication}\\
&={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus 0}}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{v_{3}}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n-1})\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}(n-1)}}(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\rrbracket))&&\textsf{Compiling maps to multilinear maps}\\
&=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket\Bigl({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus 0}}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{v_{3}}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n-1})\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}(n-1)}}(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\rrbracket)\Bigr)&&\textsf{Compiling maps to multilinear maps}\\
&=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket\Bigl(\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}{v_{3}}}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n})\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket^{{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n}}(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\rrbracket)\Bigr)&&\textsf{Because }{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus 0}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n-1})={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}f}}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}n})\\[5.0pt]
&=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{iter}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{1}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}y}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v_{3}})}\rrbracket)&&\textsf{By compiling}\\
&=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e_{2}}\rrbracket(\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v_{\textsf{n}}}\rrbracket)&&\textsf{By induction}\\
&=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\{y\mapsto v_{\textsf{n}}\}(e_{2})}\rrbracket&&\textsf{Compiling commutes with substitution}\\
&=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\rrbracket&&\textsf{By induction}\end{aligned}

The argument introduces two key lemmas, used across cases. The first states our compiler produces multilinear maps, as indicated in Fig.
13
.

Lemma

4:
(Compiler maps programs to multilinear maps)

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta,x\!:\!\tau_{1}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau_{2}}
,
then

\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{\sigma}},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{1}}\hskip 0.50003pt\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{x_{1}}}\hskip 0.50003pt\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}+}\,\,\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{2}}\hskip 0.50003pt\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{x_{2}}})={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{1}}\hskip 0.50003pt\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\hskip 0.50003pt\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{\sigma}},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{x_{1}}})\hskip 0.50003pt\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}+}\,\,\hskip 0.50003pt{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\alpha_{2}}\hskip 0.50003pt\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\cdot}\,\hskip 0.50003pt\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{\sigma}},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{x_{2}}})\vskip 1.99997pt

The second is a substitution lemma, typical of compiler correctness proofs. Both are shown by structural induction on
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}
. Appendix B.7/B.10 contain detailed proofs.

Lemma

5:
(Compiler commutes with substitution )

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta,x\!:\!\tau_{1}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau_{2}}
,
then

\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\{x\mapsto v\}(e)}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{\sigma}})=\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{\sigma}},\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}v}\rrbracket)

These are the essential arguments in establishing the correctness of our compiler. Now we address the empirical question, as to whether the correctness of our compiler yields any benefit to differentiable programming.

5.
Experiments

Our experiments test whether programming neural networks with
Cajal
({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})
helps them learn tasks with discrete structure, and whether the correctness theorem we proved is actually driving this benefit. Programming a neural network with
Cajal
({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})
means that we specify
part
of the neural network using an
open
7
7

7

Open programs have free variables.

Cajal
({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})
program, which compiles to a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}k}
-linear map implemented in a differentiable programming language. When programming neural networks this way, the equations defining their behavior can look as follows—a composition of matrices, nonlinear functions, and compiled
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}k}
-linear maps.

{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\eta}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}x})={\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}W_{2}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\textsf{relu}}\circ\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}e}\rrbracket\circ{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\textsf{relu}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}W_{1}}

Our implementation of
Cajal
({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})
is a simple prototype built in
PyTorch
, a domain-specific language for programming neural networks in
Python

(
Paszke et al. 2019
)
. It extends
PyTorch
with the primitives in
Cajal
({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\multimap},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\mathbb{N}})
, enriching it with first-class iteration and conditionals. The compiler, which implements the specification in Fig.
13
, is roughly 200 lines of
Python
.

The experiments study two related image transformation tasks. The first is the iterative image transformation task from Fig.
1
, where a neural network is programmed to solve it using first-class iteration. The second experiment is a conditional and iterative image transformation—if the input is an even digit, apply vertical blur iteratively, else return the original image. A neural network is programmed to solve it using first-class iteration
and
conditionals. These experiments illustrate, but do not exhaust, possible approaches to these tasks. They are meant to highlight the benefits and challenges inherent to differentiable programming with first-class discrete structure.

To briefly summarize our results, we find that programming neural networks with first-class discrete structure helps them learn faster and with greater data-efficiency relative to a neural network programmed without first-class iteration. These benefits persist even when using the compiler in an alternative
type-preserving
way—returning instead a random
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}k}
-linear map from
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}\rrbracket
to
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\tau}\rrbracket
. The data also reveal an interesting limitation of our specification. Linking with vectors of large
norm
can destabilize learning dynamics. Yet it is allowed by our specification, which only demands that these vectors are in
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\Delta}\rrbracket
. By bounding the norm of linked vectors, a compiler could feasibly prevent this instability.

Appendix C.1-4 contains 4 additional experiments, which replicate experiments from prior work on differentiable programming with first-class conditionals
(
Velez-Ginorio et al. 2026
)
. Since our specification and implementation differ, these data were helpful to establish consistency with prior work.

5.1.
Experiment 1: Iterative image transformation

5.1.1.
Task

Our first experiment studies the iterative image transformation task from Fig.
1
.

\triangleright

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}
is the digit
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}0}
, return
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}

\triangleright

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}
is the digit
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}
, apply vertical blur
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n}
times to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}

5.1.2.
Models

Each model is a neural network implemented in
PyTorch
, and trained to solve the iterative image transformation.

Model D
is a neural network similar to Fig.
2
. The iterative structure is
directly
programmed using our compiler.

\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{iter}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}y}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{vblur}\,y}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n})}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{x}}:{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}^{784}},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\textsf{vblur}}:{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\textsf{Lin}}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}^{784}},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}^{784}}),\,{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{n}}:{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\mathbb{R}^{10}})

The program compiles to a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}3}
-linear map, which implements the dynamics of linear recurrent neurons. Its inputs
({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\textsf{vblur}},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{n}})
are determined by neural networks.
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{x}}
is the base case of iteration, and is simply the original image.
vblur
is the linear map we iteratively apply, a linear convolution whose kernel weights are determined by a 3-layer convolutional neural network.
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{n}}
controls how many times to iterate, up to 10 steps.
8
8

8

During training it is typical to assume a maximum number of steps the recurrent neural network takes through time.
It is also determined by a 3-layer convolutional neural network.

Model T
is similar to
Model D
. But it compiles a simpler program
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}\textsf{iter}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}x}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}y}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}y}\}({\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}n})}\rrbracket({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{x}}},{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{n}})
and replaces its
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}2}
-linear map with a random matrix
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}W}
.
9
9

9

The exclusion of
vblur
is necessary, otherwise the dimensionality explodes.

{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}W}({\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{x}}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}\vec{n}})

Observe that the tensor product is used to form a linear map from a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.329,0.576}2}
-linear map. It is
type-preserving
because it keeps the type translation from our compiler. However, the random matrix
{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.14}W}
does not generally satisfy the compiler correctness theorem.

Model I
is a 3-layer convolutional neural network, a simple baseline from which to compare the impacts of programming with and without first-class iteration. It is not directly programmed with any iterative structure, thus we say it is programmed
indirectly
to solve the task.

Further details on model parameters are available in the artifact.

5.1.3.
Measures

The experiments take several measures of each model to help distinguish their learning dynamics. These feature prominently in Figs.
14
,
15
,
16
, and
17
.

The first measure is the model loss on the training and test set. These models learn by iterative adjustments of their parameters in order to minimize a loss function. The model loss helps us see if model training converges, and to what extent this convergence is stable. However, models can have similar loss curves while behaving in distinct ways. Therefore we use an additional measure that helps distinguish model behavior.

The second measure of learning dynamics is the peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) between a model output and the target output specified in the test set. It is a kind of image distance, and is a useful measure for understanding actual model behavior
(
Hore and Ziou 2010
)
. Qualitatively distinct behaviors correlate well with distinct PSNR curves. Higher PSNR indicates two images are
more
similar.

5.1.4.
Results

Each model was trained to approximate the image transformation specified in a training dataset of 60,000 image inputs and corresponding outputs. They were then tested on a distinct dataset of 10,000 image pairs. Using
L1
loss, the
Adam
optimizer computed parameter updates to each model
(
Kingma 2014
)
. During training, the previously described measures were recorded at regular intervals. This was done for 10 random seeds, 3 learning rates, and 3 training batch sizes.

Fig.
14
presents the typical observed dynamics of learning for each model. On the
x
-axis, a step indicates a parameter update to the models. The
y
-axis contains previously discussed measures, in addition to model outputs across training time. For model outputs, the first image on the left is the input. The data for each seed is shown. To understand both early and late training dynamics, the left-half of the figure focuses on the first
200
training steps, and the right-half on the first 10,000.

These results show that
Model D
and
Model T
learn to iteratively apply the image transformation. But
Model I
does not. It instead applies a more uniform blur to digits. For reference, the outputs specified in the test set for these inputs are in Fig.
1
.

At a finer level,
Model D
and
Model T
learn comparably well, observed by their outputs and PSNR. However, they arrive at similar solutions in distinct ways.
Model D
first learns to reconstruct the original image, and then to iteratively apply blur.
Model T
instead slowly learns to iteratively blur the original image without passing through a stage where it reconstructs the original image. Interestingly,
Model D
is less stable in its early training dynamics. When
vblur
has a large norm, its iterative application explodes model outputs. Before learning to produce appropriate values of
vblur
,
Model T
encounters these unstable inputs early in training.

Overall, model performance is generally consistent across learning rate and training batch size. Fig.
15
shows this using PSNR, the measure which best correlates with model performance.

. The only difference is that the iterative image transform only applies if the input digit
